.

Let
f:X\to(-\infty,\infty]
satisfy
f_{n}\uparrow f
with
f_{n}\in L^{1}
.
Then
\displaystyle\overline{I}(f)=\lim_{n\to\infty}I^{1}(f_{n})
.

As an almost version of convergence theorem, we record here the following.

Theorem 6.6

(Dominated Series Convergence)
.

Let
(f_{n})_{n\geq 1}
be a sequence of integrable functions with
f_{n}
dominated by
g_{n}\in L^{1}
so that

|f_{n}|\leq g_{n}
and
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}I^{1}(g_{n})<\infty
.
Then
\sum_{n\geq 1}f_{n}(x)
converges absolutely to an integrable function
f(x)
for almost all
x
and

I^{1}(f)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}I^{1}(f_{n})
.

Proof.

Since the increasing sequence
(\sum_{k=1}^{n}g_{k})
in
L^{1}
satisfies

\lim_{n\to\infty}I^{1}\Bigl(\sum_{k=1}^{n}g_{k}\Bigr)=\sum_{n\geq 1}I^{1}(g_{n})<\infty,

letting
g=\sum_{n\geq 1}g_{n}\in L^{1}_{\uparrow}
,
[g=\infty]
is a null set
with
[g<\infty]g
and
[g<\infty]f_{n}\mathring{=}f_{n}
integrable.
Then
f\equiv[g<\infty]\sum_{n\geq 1}f_{n}
is integrable and

\displaystyle I^{1}(f)

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}I^{1}\Bigl([g<\infty]\sum_{k=1}^{n}f_{k}\Bigr)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{n}I^{1}([g<\infty]f_{k})

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{n}I^{1}(f_{k})=\sum_{n\geq 1}I^{1}(f_{n})

by the dominated convergence theorem.
∎

Example 6.7
.

For
s>1
,
t^{s-1}/(e^{t}-1)
is an integrable function of
t>0
and we have

\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{t^{s-1}}{e^{t}-1}\,dt=\zeta(s)\Gamma(s),

where the zeta function

\zeta(s)
is defined by

\zeta(s)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n^{s}}.

In fact, the dominated series convergece (of ordinary version)
is applied to the expression
t^{s-1}/(e^{t}-1)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}t^{s-1}e^{-t}e^{-nt}
to have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{t^{s-1}}{e^{t}-1}\,dt=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{s-1}e^{-t}e^{-nt}\,dt

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{(n+1)^{s}}\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{s-1}e^{-t}\,dt

\displaystyle=\zeta(s)\Gamma(s).

At this point, we have various monotone extensions
of
L
between
L^{1}\cap L_{\uparrow}
and
L^{1}_{\uparrow}=(L^{1})_{\uparrow}
:

\displaystyle\{f\in L_{\uparrow};I_{\uparrow}(f)<\infty\},

\displaystyle\{f:X\to\text{\ym R};f\,\mathring{\in}\,L_{\uparrow}\},

\displaystyle\{f:X\to\text{\ym R};f\,\mathring{\in}\,L_{\uparrow},I^{1}_{\uparrow}(f)<\infty\}

and so on.
Among these, the last one is interesting because every integrable function is a difference of functions belonging to this class
(see Appendix C), whereas the first one is practically useful because concrete integrable functions are differences of
functions in this class, which shall be utilized in repeated integrals discussed in the next section.

7.
Repeated Integrals Revisited

Historically a reasonable formulation of the subject had not been apparent for a while and
it was crucial to allow exceptional points which constitute a null set.
We here present a practical form of the so-called Fubini theorem without getting involved much in measurability.

Let
d=d^{\prime}+d^{\prime\prime}
and express
x\in\text{\ym R}^{d}
by
x=(x^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime})
with
x^{\prime}\in\text{\ym R}^{d^{\prime}}
and
x^{\prime\prime}\in\text{\ym R}^{d^{\prime\prime}}
.
For
A\subset\text{\ym R}^{d}
, let
A^{\prime}\subset\text{\ym R}^{d^{\prime}}
(
A^{\prime\prime}\subset\text{\ym R}^{d^{\prime\prime}}
) be
the projection
of
A
to the
d^{\prime}
-component (
d^{\prime\prime}
-component) respectively and
the slice
of
A
by
x^{\prime}\in\text{\ym R}^{d^{\prime}}
(
x^{\prime\prime}\in\text{\ym R}^{d^{\prime\prime}}
) is defined to be

A_{x^{\prime}}=\{a^{\prime\prime}\in\text{\ym R}^{d^{\prime\prime}};(x^{\prime},a^{\prime\prime})\in A\}
(
A_{x^{\prime\prime}}=\{a^{\prime}\in\text{\ym R}^{d^{\prime}};(a^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime})\in A\}
).
Thus
A^{\prime}=\{x^{\prime}\in\text{\ym R}^{d^{\prime}};A_{x^{\prime}}\not=\emptyset\}
.

Note that, for an open set
U
, slices
U_{x^{\prime}}
,
U_{x^{\prime\prime}}
as well as projections
U^{\prime}
,
U^{\prime\prime}
are open sets.

Figure 9.
Projection and Slice

Proposition
3.4
is here paraphrased as follows.

Lemma 7.1
.

Let
f\in S_{\uparrow}(\text{\ym R}^{d})
. Then, for each
x^{\prime}\in\text{\ym R}^{d^{\prime}}
,
f(x^{\prime},\cdot)\in S_{\uparrow}(\text{\ym R}^{d^{\prime\prime}})
and

\int f(x^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime})\,dx^{\prime\prime}
belongs to
S_{\uparrow}(\text{\ym R}^{d^{\prime}})
as a function of
x^{\prime}
in such a way that

\int f(x)\,dx=\int dx^{\prime}\,\int f(x^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime})\,dx^{\prime\prime}.

Proposition 7.2
.

Let
f
be a real-valued function
f
on an open subset
U\subset\text{\ym R}^{d}
and assume that
f_{\pm}=0\vee(\pm f)\in S_{\uparrow}^{+}(U)
.
Recall that the condition
f_{\pm}\in S_{\uparrow}^{+}(U)
holds if
f\in C(U)
(Proposition
5.2
).
The zero-extension of
f
to
\text{\ym R}^{d}
is here denoted by
Uf
.

Then
Uf
is Lebesgue integrable if and only if
|f|\in S_{\uparrow}^{+}(U)
fulfills

I_{\uparrow}(U|f|)=I_{\uparrow}(Uf_{+})+I_{\uparrow}(Uf_{-})<\infty
, i.e.,

\int_{U^{\prime}}dx^{\prime}\int_{U_{x^{\prime}}}|f(x^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime})|\,dx^{\prime\prime}<\infty.

Moreover, if this is satisfied, we have

\int_{U}f(x)\,dx=\int_{U^{\prime}}dx^{\prime}\int_{U_{x^{\prime}}}f(x^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime})\,dx^{\prime\prime}.

Here
f(x^{\prime},\cdot)
belongs to
L^{1}(\text{\ym R}^{d^{\prime\prime}})
for almost all
x^{\prime}\in U^{\prime}
and

\displaystyle\int_{U_{x^{\prime}}}f(x^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime})\,dx^{\prime\prime}
is integrable as a function of
x^{\prime}\in U^{\prime}
.

Proof.

By Proposition
5.2
(iv) and Lemma
7.1
, the integrability of
Uf
is equivalent to the condition that

|f|\in S_{\uparrow}^{+}(U)
satisfies

\int_{U^{\prime}}dx^{\prime}\int_{U_{x^{\prime}}}|f(x^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime})|\,dx^{\prime\prime}=I_{\uparrow}(U|f|)=I_{\uparrow}(Uf_{+})+I_{\uparrow}(Uf_{-})<\infty.

Moreover, under this condition,

\displaystyle\int_{U}f(x)\,dx

\displaystyle=\int_{U}f_{+}(x)\,dx-\int_{U}f_{-}(x)\,dx

\displaystyle=\int_{U^{\prime}}dx^{\prime}\int_{U_{x^{\prime}}}f_{+}(x^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime})\,dx^{\prime\prime}-\int_{U^{\prime}}dx^{\prime}\int_{U_{x^{\prime}}}f_{-}(x^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime})\,dx^{\prime\prime}.

In this expression, Corollary
6.5
is used to see that
\int_{U}f_{\pm}(dx)\,dx<\infty
implies

f_{\pm}(x^{\prime},\cdot)\in L^{1}(\text{\ym R}^{d^{\prime\prime}})
, i.e.,

f(x^{\prime},\cdot)=f_{+}(x^{\prime},\cdot)-f_{-}(x^{\prime},\cdot)\in L^{1}(\text{\ym R}^{d^{\prime\prime}})
for almost all
x^{\prime}\in U^{\prime}
(as functions on
\text{\ym R}^{d^{\prime\prime}}
supported by
U_{x^{\prime}}
)
and

\displaystyle\int_{U^{\prime}}dx^{\prime}\int_{U_{x^{\prime}}}f_{+}(x^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime})\,dx^{\prime\prime}-\int_{U^{\prime}}dx^{\prime}\int_{U_{x^{\prime}}}f_{-}(x^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime})\,dx^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=\int_{U^{\prime}}dx^{\prime}\int_{U_{x^{\prime}}}f(x^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime})\,dx^{\prime\prime}.

∎

Corollary 7.3
.

Let
\varphi<\psi
be
\overline{\text{\ym R}}
-valued continuous functions on an open subset
U\subset\text{\ym R}^{d}
and

D=\{(x,y);x\in U,\varphi(x)<y<\psi(x)\}\in S_{\uparrow}(\text{\ym R}^{d+1})
be an open set bordered by
\varphi
and
\psi
(a graph region
).

Then, for a real-valued function
f
on
D
satisfying
f_{\pm}\in S_{\uparrow}^{+}(D)
,

D|f|\in S_{\uparrow}(\text{\ym R}^{d+1})
and its integral
I_{\uparrow}(D|f|)
is calculated by

\int_{D}|f|=\int_{U}dx\,\int_{\varphi(x)}^{\psi(x)}|f(x,y)|\,dy

so that this is finite if and only if
Df\in L^{1}(\text{\ym R}^{d+1})
. Moreover, if this is the case,

I^{1}(Df)=\int_{D}f=\int_{U}dx\,\int_{\varphi(x)}^{\psi(x)}f(x,y)\,dy,

In particular, for the choice
f\equiv 1
and
U=(a,b)
,
the Lebesgue measure
|D|
of
D
is expressed by a one-variable integral

|D|=\int_{a}^{b}(\psi(x)-\varphi(x))\,dx,

which is exactly the area formula for
D
in the elementary calculus.

Figure 10.
Graph Region

Remark 9
.

Repeated integral furmulas discussed so far are further extended to integrable functions, known as Fubini’s theorem.
Our formulation is just restricted to
L_{\updownarrow}
in essence, which enables us to avoid almost-technicalities.

One of most efficient way to circumvent these is to rephrase the integrability according to Appendix
C
and write down

M
-integrability in repeated integrals.

Example 7.4
.

When
U
is bounded and
\phi:U\to\text{\ym R}
is a continuous function,
the choice
\varphi=\phi-\delta
and
\psi=\phi+\delta
with
\delta>0
gives

|D|=\int_{U}2\delta\,dx=2\delta|U|\downarrow 0\quad(\delta\downarrow 0).

Thus the graph
\{(x,\phi(x));x\in U\}\subset U\times\text{\ym R}
of
\phi
, which is included in
D
for any
\delta>0
, is a null set.
This property remains valid even for an unbounded
U
as a countable union of bounded graphs.

Example 7.5
.

Consider the repeated integral of
e^{-(1+x^{2})y}
supported by the first quadrant
(0,\infty)^{2}\subset\text{\ym R}^{2}
.
In terms of the half Gaussian integral

C=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-x^{2}}\,dx
, this is

\int_{x>0,y>0}e^{-(1+x^{2})y}\,dxdy=\int_{0}^{\infty}dy\,e^{-y}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-yx^{2}}\,dx=C\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-y}\frac{1}{\sqrt{y}}\,dy=2C^{2},

which is equal to

\int_{0}^{\infty}dx\,\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-(1+x^{2})y}\,dy=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{x^{2}+1}\,dx=\frac{\pi}{2}.

Thus

\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-x^{2}}\,dx=\frac{\sqrt{\pi}}{2}.

Example 7.6

(Dirichlet integral)
.

From repeated integrals of the double integral
\displaystyle\int_{x>0,y>r}e^{-xy}\sin x\,dxdy
(
r>0
), we have

\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-rx}\frac{\sin x}{x}\,dx=\frac{\pi}{2}-\arctan r,

which is the Laplace transform of
\sinc(x)=(\sin x)/x
(
x>0
) and its continuity at
r=+0
(Theorem
2.27
)
results in the formula

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\sin x}{x}\,dx=\frac{\pi}{2}.

Exercise 31
.

Compute
\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{(\sin x)^{2n+1}}{x}\,dx
for
n=1,2,\dots
.

In Proposition
7.2
, ‘almost all’ qualification appeared instead of ‘all’, which is really needed.

Example 7.7
.

Let
U=\{(x,y)\in\text{\ym R}^{2};0<x^{2}+y^{2}<1\}
and consider a continuous function
f
on
U
defined by

f(x,y)=1/(x^{2}+y^{2})^{\alpha/2}
(
\alpha>0
). Then,

\int_{-\sqrt{1-x^{2}}}^{\sqrt{1-x^{2}}}f(x,y)\,dy=2\int_{0}^{\sqrt{1-x^{2}}}\frac{1}{(x^{2}+y^{2})^{\alpha/2}}\,dy\quad(x\not=0)

and

\int_{-1}^{1}f(0,y)\,dy=2\int_{0}^{1}\frac{1}{y^{\alpha}}\,dy=\begin{cases}2/(1-\alpha)&(\alpha<1)\\
\infty&(\alpha\geq 1)\end{cases}.

Thus the partial integral
\int f(x,y)\,dy
is not integrable at
x=0
if
\alpha\geq 1
.

Even in that case,
\int f(x,y)\,dy
is continuous in
-1\leq x\leq 1
as a
[0,\infty]
-valued function and
one sees that

\int_{-1}^{1}dx\,\int_{-\sqrt{1-x^{2}}}^{\sqrt{1-x^{2}}}f(x,y)\,dy=\int_{U}f(x,y)\,dxdy,

which turns out to be finite if and only if
\alpha<2
(see Example
8.10
).

Exercise 32
.

Show that
\int_{-\sqrt{1-x^{2}}}^{\sqrt{1-x^{2}}}f(x,y)\,dy
is continuous as a
[0,\infty]
-valued function of
x\in[-1,1]
.

As a theoretical application, we take up the integration-by-parts formula for improper integrals.
Let
f
and
g
be real-valued functions on an open interval
(a,b)
satisfying the condition that

[x,y]f,[x,y]g\in S_{\uparrow}(\text{\ym R})\cap S_{\downarrow}(\text{\ym R})
for
[x,y]\subset(a,b)
.
Let
F
and
G
be indefinite integrals of
f
and
g
respectively, i.e.,
F
and
G
are continuous functions on
(a,b)
satisfying

\int_{x}^{y}f(t)\,dt=F(y)-F(x),\quad\int_{x}^{y}g(t)\,dt=G(y)-G(x)

for any
[x,y]\subset(a,b)
.

We assume that the improper integrals of
f
and
g
exist, i.e.,
F
and
G
are continuously extended to
[a,b]
so that

F(a)=\lim_{x\to a+0}F(x),\quad F(b)=\lim_{x\to b-0}F(x)

and similarly for
G
.

For a subinterval
[x,y]\subset(a,b)
,
[x,y]G\in S_{\uparrow}(\text{\ym R})\cap S_{\downarrow}(\text{\ym R})
by Corollary
2.7
and then

[x,y]fG\in S_{\uparrow}(\text{\ym R})\cap S_{\downarrow}(\text{\ym R})
by Proposition
2.5
(iii).
Thus we can talk about improper integrability of product functions
fG
and
Fg
.

Proposition 7.8
.

The improper integrals of
fG
and
Fg
exist if one of them exists and, if this is the case, we have

\int_{a}^{b}F(t)g(t)\,dt+\int_{a}^{b}f(t)G(t)\,dt=F(b)G(b)-F(a)G(a).

Proof.

Let
h=f\otimes g
, i.e.,
h(s,t)=f(s)g(t)
(
s,t\in(a,b)
) with

h_{+}=f_{+}\otimes g_{+}+f_{-}\otimes g_{-},\quad h_{-}=f_{+}\otimes g_{-}+f_{-}\otimes g_{+}.

In view of
[x,y]f_{\pm}=[x,y](|f|\pm f),[x,y]g_{\pm}=[x,y](|g|\pm g)\in S_{\uparrow}^{+}(\text{\ym R})
for
[x,y]\subset(a,b)
,
a bounded open set
D=\{(s,t);x<s<t<y\}
satisfies

Dh_{\pm}=D([x,y]\times[x,y])h_{\pm}\in S_{\uparrow}^{+}(D)
by Proposition
5.2
(ii).
Since
h
is bounded on
[x,y]\times[x,y]
,
Dh
as well as
Dh_{\pm}
is integrable and

\displaystyle\int_{D}h(s,t)\,dsdt
is calculated in two ways by Corollary
7.3
to have

\displaystyle\int_{x}^{y}dsf(s)\int_{s}^{y}dtg(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{x}^{y}dsf(s)(G(y)-G(s))

\displaystyle=(F(y)-F(x))G(y)-\int_{x}^{y}f(s)G(s)\,ds

and

\displaystyle\int_{x}^{y}dtg(t)\int_{x}^{t}f(s)\,ds

\displaystyle=\int_{x}^{y}dtg(t)(F(t)-F(x))

\displaystyle=\int_{x}^{y}F(t)g(t)\,dt-F(x)(G(y)-G(x)),

whence

\int_{x}^{y}f(s)G(s)\,ds+\int_{x}^{y}F(t)g(t)\,dt=F(y)G(y)-F(x)G(x).

The assertion is now obtained by taking limits
x\to a
and
y\to b
.
∎

Remark 10
.

When repeated integrals are fully developped, the integration-by-parts formula can be generalized to locally integrable functions.

Exercise 33
.

A continuous function
f
in Theorem
2.27
can be replaced with one satisfying
[x,y]f\in S_{\uparrow}(\text{\ym R})\cap S_{\downarrow}(\text{\ym R})

for
[x,y]\subset(0,\infty)
.

8.
Jacobian Formula

To fully appreciate the power of repeated integrals, we here establish the change-of-variables formula in multiple integrals.

Theorem 8.1

(Transfer Principle)
.

Let
(L,I)
,
(M,J)
be integral systems on sets
X
,
Y
respectively,
\rho:X\to[0,\infty)
be a function on
X
and

\phi:X\to Y
be a map satisfying
\rho(M\circ\phi)\subset L
and
I(\rho(g\circ\phi))=J(g)
(
g\in M
).
Here
\rho(g\circ\phi)
denotes the product function of
\rho
and
g\circ\phi
.

Then we have
\rho(M^{1}\circ\phi)\subset L^{1}
and

I^{1}(\rho(g\circ\phi))=J^{1}(g)
(
g\in M^{1}
) for their Daniell extensions.

Proof.

We just check
\rho(M_{\uparrow}\circ\phi)\subset L_{\uparrow}
,

I_{\uparrow}(\rho(g\circ\phi))=J_{\uparrow}(g)
(
g\in M_{\uparrow}
) and so on, step by step.
Details are left to the reader.
∎

Corollary 8.2
.

(i)

If
\phi:X\to Y
is bijective and

L=M\circ\phi
, we have

L^{1}=M^{1}\circ\phi
and
J^{1}(g)=I^{1}(g\circ\phi)

(
g\in M^{1}
).

(ii)

If integral systems
(L,I)
,
(M,J)
on a set
X

satisfy
L\subset M
,
J|_{L}=I
, i.e.,

(M,J)
is an extension of
(L,I)
, then

L^{1}\subset M^{1}
and
J^{1}
on
M^{1}
is an extension of
I^{1}
on
L^{1}
.

Example 8.3
.

For
f\in L^{1}(\text{\ym R}^{d})
and
y\in\text{\ym R}^{d}
,

f(x+y)
is integrable as a function of
x\in\text{\ym R}^{d}
and

\int_{\text{\ym R}^{d}}f(x+y)\,dx=\int_{\text{\ym R}^{d}}f(x)\,dx.

This follows from translational invariance of the volume integral.

Exercise 34
.

For a function
f\in L^{1}(\text{\ym R}^{d})
and
a positive real
r>0
, check the identity

\int_{\text{\ym R}^{d}}f(rx)\,dx=r^{-d}\int_{\text{\ym R}^{d}}f(x)\,dx.

We now state our goal (Jacobian
12
12

12

Recall that the Jacobian of a smooth change-of-variables
y=\phi(x)
is

\det(\phi^{\prime}(x))
, which is also denoted by
\frac{\partial(y_{1},\dots,y_{d})}{\partial(x_{1},\dots,x_{d})}
.
formula
)
in this section as follows.

Theorem 8.4
.

Let
U
,
V
be open subsets of
\text{\ym R}^{d}
and
\phi:U\to V
be a
smooth change-of-variables, i.e.,
\phi
is bijective with

\phi
and
\phi^{-1}
differentiable and the derivative
\phi^{\prime}:U\to M_{d}(\text{\ym R})
of
\phi
continuous.
Note that
\phi^{\prime}(x)
is an invertible matrix for each
x\in U
.

Then, for
g\in C_{c}(V)\cup C^{+}(V)
,

\int_{V}g(y)\,dy=\int_{U}g(\phi(x))|\det(\phi^{\prime}(x))|\,dx.

Thanks to the transfer principle, the Jacobian formula remains valid for
g\in L^{1}(V)
as well.

Corollary 8.5
.

A function
g
on
V
is Lebesgue integrable (Legesgue negligible)
if and only if so is
(g\circ\phi)|\det(\phi^{\prime})|
on
U
(
g\circ\phi
on
U
).

Remark 11
.

Nowadays, there seems some confusion in what Jacobian
means. In view of historical flow,
it was used (and is still used) to express
\det(\phi^{\prime}(x))

but a recent usage is widened to refer to its absolute value as well or even the differential matrix
\phi^{\prime}(x)
.

Proposition 8.6
.

A smooth change-of-variables preserves Lebesgue measurable sets as well as Lebesgue null sets.

Proof.

Let
\phi:U\to V
be a smooth change-of-variables and
B\subset V
be Lebesgue measurable.
By Proposition
4.17
we have an expression
B_{m}\uparrow B
with
(B_{m})_{m\geq 1}
an increasing sequence of Lebesgue integrable sets.
Since
|\det\phi^{\prime}|^{-1}
is a continuous function on
U
, we can find a sequence
h_{n}\in C_{c}^{+}(U)
so that
h_{n}\uparrow|\det\phi^{\prime}|^{-1}
.
Then

(B_{m}\circ\phi)|\det\phi^{\prime}|h_{n}\in L^{1}(U)C_{c}(U)\subset L^{1}(U)

(Proposition
5.6
) and
h_{n}(B_{m}\circ\phi)|\det\phi^{\prime}|\uparrow B_{m}\circ\phi
shows that
B_{m}\circ\phi\in L^{1}_{\uparrow}(U)
, whence

B_{m}\circ\phi\uparrow B\circ\phi\in L^{1}_{\uparrow}(U)
by Lemma
4.9
.
Thus
B\circ\phi=\phi^{-1}(B)
is Lebesgue measurable by Proposition
4.17
(i).

For a null set
B
,

\int_{U}(B\circ\phi)|\det\phi^{\prime}|=\int_{V}B=|B|=0

and
\phi^{-1}(B)=[(B\circ\phi)|\det\phi^{\prime}|>0]
is a null set by Proposition
6.1
(iii).
∎

Corollary 8.7
.

Let
U
be an open subset of
\text{\ym R}^{d}
and
\phi:U\to\text{\ym R}
be a smooth function satisfying
\phi^{\prime}(x)\not=0
(
x\in U
).
Then each level set
[\phi=c]
is negligible relative to the volume integral.

Example 8.8
.

For
\alpha>0
and
\beta>0
, consider the integral

\int_{\text{\ym R}^{2}}\frac{e^{-\beta(x^{2}+y^{2})}}{|x^{2}+y^{2}-1|^{\alpha}}\,dxdy.

whose integrand has singularity on the circle
x^{2}+y^{2}=1
. Since this unit circle is negligible as a level set of
x^{2}+y^{2}
,
the integral as well as integrability is reduced to

\int_{x^{2}+y^{2}\not=1}\frac{e^{-\beta(x^{2}+y^{2})}}{|x^{2}+y^{2}-1|^{\alpha}}\,dxdy,

which is an integral of continuous function on an open subset.

Proof of Jacobian Formula

We first deal with the special case when
\phi:\text{\ym R}^{d}\to\text{\ym R}^{d}
is realized by a matrix multiplication:
Let
T
be an invertible matrix of size
d
. Then for
f\in C_{c}(\text{\ym R}^{d})
and hence for
f\in L^{1}(\text{\ym R}^{d})
by the transfer principle,

\int f(Tx)\,dx=|\det T|^{-1}\int f(x)\,dx,

whence
|T^{-1}A|=|\det T|^{-1}|A|
for a Lebesgue integrable set
A
of
\text{\ym R}^{d}
.
Remark here that under an invertible linear transformation of variables
C_{c}(\text{\ym R}^{d})\subset S_{\uparrow}(\text{\ym R}^{d})\cap S_{\downarrow}(\text{\ym R}^{d})
is invariant,
whereas
S_{\uparrow}(\text{\ym R}^{d})\cap S_{\downarrow}(\text{\ym R}^{d})
is not as noticed before.

Since any invertible matrix is a product of elementary ones and the volume integral is permutation-invariant,
the repeated integral formula on
S_{\uparrow}(\text{\ym R}^{d})\cap S_{\downarrow}(\text{\ym R}^{d})
reduces the problem to checking it for two-dimensional matrices

\begin{pmatrix}\alpha&0\\
0&\beta\end{pmatrix},\quad\begin{pmatrix}1&\gamma\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},

where
\alpha,\beta\in\text{\ym R}^{\times}
and
\gamma\in\text{\ym R}
.

For these,
the scale covariance and the translational invariance of the width integral are combined with repeated integrals to conclude as follows:

\int_{\text{\ym R}^{2}}f(\alpha x,\beta y)\,dxdy=\frac{1}{|\alpha||\beta|}\int_{\text{\ym R}^{2}}f(x,y)\,dxdy.

\displaystyle\int_{\text{\ym R}^{2}}f(x+\gamma y,y)\,dxdy

\displaystyle=\int_{\text{\ym R}}dy\int_{\text{\ym R}}f(x+\gamma y,y)\,dx

\displaystyle(\text{by the translational invariance of $\int dx$})

\displaystyle=\int_{\text{\ym R}}dy\int_{\text{\ym R}}f(x,y)\,dx=\int_{\text{\ym R}^{2}}f(x,y)\,dxdy.

Next we go on to the non-linear case after J. Schwartz
[
6
]
.
For the Jacobian formula on
C_{c}(V)
, it is enough to show the validity for
g\in C_{c}^{+}(V)
,
which in turn implies the case
C^{+}(V)
because each
g\in C^{+}(V)
is expressed in the form
g_{n}\uparrow g
with
g_{n}\in C_{c}^{+}(V)
.

To establish the formula on
C_{c}^{+}(V)
, we need some notations in norm estimates.
For a numerical vector
x=(x_{1},\dots,x_{d})\in\text{\ym R}^{d}
and a real matrix
A=(a_{i,j})_{1\leq i,j\leq d}
, set

\|x\|_{\infty}=\max_{1\leq i\leq d}\{|x_{i}|\},\quad\|A\|=\max_{1\leq i\leq d}\{\sum_{j=1}^{d}|a_{i,j}|\},

where
\|A\|
is the operator norm relative to
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
and satisfies inequalities

\|Ax\|_{\infty}\leq\|A\|\|x\|_{\infty}
,
\|AB\|\leq\|A\|\|B\|
.

Exercise 35
.

Check these inequalities.

Let
[a,b]
(
b_{1}-a_{1}=\dots=b_{d}-a_{d}=2r
) be a
d
-dimensional closed cube contained in
U
.
By the fundamental formula in calculus, we have

\phi(x)-\phi(c)=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\int_{0}^{1}dt\,(x_{j}-c_{j})\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial x_{j}}(tx+(1-t)c)

and then

\|\phi(x)-\phi(c)\|_{\infty}\leq\|x-c\|_{\infty}\max_{0\leq t\leq 1}\|\phi^{\prime}(tx+(1-t)c)\|

for
x,c\in[a,b]
.

In particular, choosing
c=(a+b)/2
,
we see that
\phi([a,b])
is included in the closed cube of center
\phi(c)
and width
2r\|\phi^{\prime}\|_{[a,b]}
, whence

|\phi((a,b])|\leq\|\phi^{\prime}\|_{[a,b]}^{d}(2r)^{d}=\|\phi^{\prime}\|_{[a,b]}^{d}|(a,b]|,

where, for a subset
C\subset U
,

\|\phi^{\prime}\|_{C}=\sup\{\|\phi^{\prime}(x)\|;x\in C\}
.
Recall here that
\phi((a,b])
is a Lebesgue integrable set (Example
5.8
).

Invoking the chain rule
(\phi^{\prime}(c)^{-1}\phi)^{\prime}=\phi^{\prime}(c)^{-1}\phi^{\prime}
,
the above estimate applied to a map
\phi^{\prime}(c)^{-1}\phi:U\to\phi^{\prime}(c)^{-1}(V)
takes the form

|\phi((a,b])|=|\det\phi^{\prime}(c)||\phi^{\prime}(c)^{-1}\phi((a,b])|\leq|\det\phi^{\prime}(c)||\phi^{\prime}(c)^{-1}\phi^{\prime}\|_{[a,b]}^{d}|(a,b]|.

Let
f=g\circ\phi\in C_{c}^{+}(U)
and divide
(a,b]
into a multiple partition
\Delta

so that
(a,b]
is a disjoint union of open-closed subcubes
(R_{i})_{1\leq i\leq m}
with width
2r
.
We then apply the above inequality for each
R_{i}
with the center
\xi_{i}
of
R_{i}
as a sample point to have

I^{1}(f_{(\Delta],\xi}\circ\phi^{-1})=\sum_{i}f(\xi_{i})|\phi(R_{i})|\leq\sum_{i}f(\xi_{i})|\det\phi^{\prime}(\xi_{i})|\|\phi^{\prime}(\xi_{i})^{-1}\phi^{\prime}\|_{R_{i}}^{d}|R_{i}|.

Note here that
\phi(R_{i})=R_{i}\circ\phi^{-1}
as an indicator function and hence
|\phi(R_{i})|=I^{1}(R_{i}\circ\phi^{-1})
.

Now, letting
m\to\infty
so that
r\to 0
,

f_{\Delta,\xi}
converges uniformly to
(a,b]f
and the dominated convergence theorem gives

\displaystyle\lim_{m\to\infty}I^{1}(f_{(\Delta],\xi}\circ\phi^{-1})

\displaystyle=I^{1}(((a,b]f)\circ\phi^{-1})

\displaystyle=I^{1}(\phi((a,b])(f\circ\phi^{-1}))=\int_{\phi((a,b])}f(\phi^{-1}(y))\,dy,

whereas, in the right hand side of the inequality,

\phi^{\prime}(\xi_{i})^{-1}\phi^{\prime}(x)
converges to the identity matrix uniformly in
i
and
x\in R_{i}
,
which is combined with the Cauchy-Riemann formula (Theorem
3.10
) to get

\lim_{m\to\infty}\sum_{i}f(\xi_{i})|\det\phi^{\prime}(\xi_{i})|\|\phi^{\prime}(\xi_{i})^{-1}\phi^{\prime}\|_{R_{i}}^{d}|R_{i}|=\int_{(a,b]}f(x)|\det\phi^{\prime}(x)|\,dx,

concluding that

\int_{\phi((a,b])}g(y)\,dy\leq\int_{(a,b]}g(\phi(x))|\det\phi^{\prime}(x)|\,dx.

Now express
U
as a disjoint union of countably many dyadic cubes
(a,b]
satisfying
[a,b]\subset U

so that
f=\sum(a,b]f
.
Since
g\in C_{c}^{+}(V)
is integrable, we can apply the dominated convergence theorem (or the dominated series convergence) to
the expression
\sum((a,b]f)\circ\phi^{-1}=f\circ\phi^{-1}=g
to obtain

\displaystyle\int_{V}g(y)\,dy=\sum_{(a,b]}\int_{\phi((a,b])}g(y)\,dy

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{(a,b]}\int_{(a,b]}g(\phi(x))|\det\phi^{\prime}(x)|\,dx

\displaystyle=\int_{U}g(\phi(x))|\det\phi^{\prime}(x)|\,dx.

Since the last integrand
h
is in
C_{c}^{+}(U)
,
\phi^{-1}:V\to U
is applied for
h
, together with
the chain rule
\phi^{\prime}(\phi^{-1}(y))(\phi^{-1})^{\prime}(y)=(\phi\circ\phi^{-1})^{\prime}(y)=\text{id}
, to obtain the reverse inequality

\int_{U}g(\phi(x))|\det\phi^{\prime}(x)|\,dx\leq\int_{V}g(y)\,dy,

proving the Jacobian formula for
g\in C_{c}^{+}(V)
.

Exercise 36
.

\phi((a,b])g
when
[a,b]\subset U
. Hint: Express
(a,b_{n})\downarrow(a,b]
and notice that
\phi(a,b_{n})g
is
integrable.

\lim_{m\to\infty}\sum_{i}f(\xi_{i})|\det\phi^{\prime}(\xi_{i})|\|\phi^{\prime}(\xi_{i})^{-1}\phi^{\prime}\|_{R_{i}}^{d}|R_{i}|=\int_{(a,b]}f(x)|\det\phi^{\prime}(x)|\,dx.

Hint: The right hand side is equal to

\displaystyle\lim_{m\to\infty}\sum_{i}f(\xi_{i})|\det(\phi^{\prime}(\xi)||R_{i}^{\circ}|
.

Remark 12
.

If we use the technique of partition of unity concerning open coverings,
we can dispense with convergence theorems in Lebesgue integrals and complete the whole proof within Cauchy-Riemann integrals.

Example 8.9
.

Let
n=2
and

\phi:(0,\infty)\times(-\pi,\pi)\to\text{\ym R}^{2}\setminus(-\infty,0]\times\{0\}
be
the polar coordinate transformation

\phi(r,\theta)=(r\cos\theta,r\sin\theta)
. Here old variables are
(r,\theta)
and we regard
(x,y)
as
new variables. Then

\det(\phi^{\prime}(r,\theta))=\begin{vmatrix}\cos\theta&-r\sin\theta\\
\sin\theta&r\cos\theta\end{vmatrix}=r

and, if an open set
U\subset(0,\infty)\times(-\pi,\pi)
is transformed into
an open set
V\subset\text{\ym R}^{2}\setminus(-\infty,0]\times\{0\}
by
\phi
, the equality

\int_{V}g(x,y)\,dxdy=\int_{U}g(r\cos\theta,r\sin\theta)\,rdrd\theta

holds for
g\in C^{+}(V)
. Thus, if
f\in C(V)
satisfies
|f|\leq g
with

\int_{U}g(r\cos\theta,r\sin\theta)\,rdrd\theta<\infty,

then
f\in L^{1}(V)
and

\int_{V}f(x,y)\,dxdy=\int_{U}f(r\cos\theta,r\sin\theta)\,rdrd\theta.

Since
\text{\ym R}^{2}\setminus V=(-\infty,0]\times\{0\}
is a null set,
f
is integrable as a function on
\text{\ym R}^{2}
and we also have

\int_{\text{\ym R}^{2}}f(x,y)\,dxdy=\int_{0}^{\infty}dr\,r\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}f(r\cos\theta,r\sin\theta)\,d\theta.

Figure 11.
Polar Coordinates

Example 8.10
.

As a supplement to Example
7.7
, we have

\int_{x^{2}+y^{2}<1}\frac{1}{(x^{2}+y^{2})^{\alpha/2}}\,dxdy=2\pi\int_{0}^{1}\frac{r}{r^{\alpha}}\,dr=\begin{cases}1/(2-\alpha)&(\alpha<2)\\
\infty&(\alpha\geq 2)\end{cases}.

Exercise 38
.

Express the integral

\int_{x^{2}+y^{2}>1}\frac{e^{-\beta(x^{2}+y^{2})}}{(x^{2}+y^{2}-1)^{\alpha}}\,dxdy

(
\alpha>0
,
\beta>0
) in Example
8.8
by the Gamma function.

Example 8.11
.

For
C=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{-t^{2}}\,dt
we have

\displaystyle C^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{\text{\ym R}^{2}}e^{-(x^{2}+y^{2})}\,dxdy=\int_{(0,\infty)\times(-\pi,\pi)}e^{-r^{2}}r\,drd\theta

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-r^{2}}r\,dr\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}d\theta=\pi\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-r^{2}}d(r^{2})=\pi,

showing
C=\sqrt{\pi}
again.

As a popular application, we shall express the beta function in terms of the gamma function.

Recall that the gamma function
is defined by

\Gamma(t)=\int_{0}^{\infty}x^{t-1}e^{-x}\,dx=2\int_{0}^{\infty}x^{2t-1}e^{x^{2}}\,dx\quad(t>0),

which is a continuous replacement of factorial in the sense that
(t-1)!=\Gamma(t)
.
The
beta function
is defined by a possibly improper integral

B(s,t)=\int_{0}^{1}x^{s-1}(1-x)^{t-1}\,dx\quad(s>0,t>0).

Exercise 39
.

These improper integrals are well-defined.

Theorem 8.12
.

The beta function is expressed by

B(s,t)=2\int_{0}^{\pi/2}\cos^{2s-1}\theta\sin^{2t-1}\theta\,d\theta.

and related to the gamma function by

B(s,t)=\frac{\Gamma(s)\Gamma(t)}{\Gamma(s+t)}.

Proof.

The expression of trigonometric integral is immediate from the variable change
x=\cos^{2}\theta
(
0\leq\theta\leq\pi/2
).

We repeat the argument of Gaussian integral in polar coordinates.

\displaystyle\Gamma(s)\Gamma(t)

\displaystyle=4\int_{0}^{\infty}x^{2s-1}e^{-x^{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}y^{2t-1}e^{-y^{2}}\,dy

\displaystyle=4\int_{(0,\infty)\times(0,\infty)}x^{2s-1}y^{2t-1}e^{-(x^{2}+y^{2})}\,dxdy

\displaystyle=4\int_{0}^{\infty}dr\,r\int_{0}^{\pi/2}r^{2(s+t)-2}e^{-r^{2}}\cos^{2s-1}\theta\sin^{2t-1}\theta\,d\theta

\displaystyle=2\int_{0}^{\infty}r^{2(s+t)-1}e^{-r^{2}}\,drB(s,t)=\Gamma(s+t)B(s,t).

∎

Exercise 40

(Dirichlet integral)
.

Let
D=\{(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in\text{\ym R}^{n};x_{1}\geq 0,\dots,x_{n}\geq 0,x_{1}+\dots+x_{n}\leq 1\}
be an
n
-dimensional simplex.
For stricly positive reals
a_{0},a_{1},\dots,a_{n}
, show that

\int_{D}x_{1}^{a_{1}-1}\dots x_{n}^{a_{n}-1}(1-x_{1}-\dots-x_{n})^{a_{0}-1}\,dx_{1}\cdots dx_{n}=\frac{\Gamma(a_{0})\Gamma(a_{1})\cdots\Gamma(a_{n})}{\Gamma(a_{0}+a_{1}+\dots+a_{n})}.

9.
Surface Integrals

As another application of the Jacobian formula, we shall describe the curvilinear extent of a geometric object such as
the length of a curve or the area of a surface.

Figure 12.
Parametrized Object

Let our geometric object
M\subset\text{\ym R}^{d}
be parametrized by coordinates
u=(u_{1},\dots,u_{m})\in U
in the form
x=\phi(u)
.
Here
U
is an open subset of
\text{\ym R}^{m}
and
\phi:U\to\text{\ym R}^{d}
is a
smooth (i.e., continuously differentiable)
injective map satisfying
\text{rank}(\phi^{\prime}(u))=m
(
u\in U
)
and
\phi(U)=M
.

For a small rectangle
\Delta u=\Delta u_{1}\times\dots\times\Delta u_{m}
inside
U
, its image under
\phi
is approximately
a parallelotope in
\text{\ym R}^{d}
spanned by vectors

|\Delta u_{1}|\partial_{1}\phi,\dots,|\Delta u_{m}|\partial_{m}\phi,\quad\partial_{i}\phi=\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial u_{i}}\in\text{\ym R}^{d}

with its
m
-dimensional volume given by
\sqrt{\det(\partial_{i}\phi|\partial_{j}\phi)}|\Delta u|
.

Exercise 41
.

Show that the
m
-dimensional volume of a parallelotope spanned by vectors
\xi_{j}\in\text{\ym R}^{d}
(
1\leq j\leq m\leq d
) is

\sqrt{\det(\xi_{i}|\xi_{j})}
. (See
[
5
, Theorem 6.2.16]
for example.)

Figure 13.
Linear Approximation

Thus it is reasonable to define the
m
-dimensional
extent
of
M
by

\int_{M}|dx|_{M}=\int_{U}\sqrt{\det(\partial_{i}\phi|\partial_{j}\phi)}\,du

with
\sqrt{\det(\partial_{i}\phi|\partial_{j}\phi)}
called the
extent density
of
\phi
.

The definition is fairly speculative but it certainly bears several desirable properties:

(i)

It correctly responds under scaling: For
r>0
,
rM
is parametrized by
r\phi
and

\sqrt{\det(\partial_{i}r\phi|\partial_{j}r\phi)}=r^{m}\sqrt{\det(\partial_{i}\phi|\partial_{j}\phi)}
shows

\int_{U}\sqrt{\det\Bigl(\frac{\partial r\phi}{\partial u_{i}}\Bigl|\frac{\partial r\phi}{\partial u_{j}}\Bigr.\Bigr)}\,du=r^{m}\int_{U}\sqrt{\det\Bigl(\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial u_{i}}\Bigl|\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial u_{j}}\Bigr.\Bigr)}\,du.

(ii)

It is invariant under Euclidean transformations in
\text{\ym R}^{d}
.
Let
T:\text{\ym R}^{d}\to\text{\ym R}^{d}
be a Euclidean transformation, then
TM
is parametrized by
T\phi
and

\det(\partial_{i}(T\phi)|\partial_{j}(T\phi))=\det(\partial_{i}\phi|\partial_{j}\phi)
gives the invariance.

(iii)

It is independent of choices of parametrization.
In fact, for another parametrization
V\ni v\mapsto\psi(v)\in M
of
M
with
V
an open subset of
\text{\ym R}^{m}
, the chain rule

\Bigl(\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial u_{i}}\Bigl|\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial u_{j}}\Bigr.\Bigr)=\sum_{k,l}\Bigl(\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial v_{k}}\Bigl|\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial v_{l}}\Bigr.\Bigr)\frac{\partial v_{k}}{\partial u_{i}}\frac{\partial v_{l}}{\partial u_{j}}

gives
13
13

13

\frac{dv}{du}=\det\frac{\partial v}{\partial u}
is the Jacobian with

\frac{\partial v}{\partial u}=(\frac{\partial v_{j}}{\partial u_{i}})
denoting the differential matrix.

\sqrt{\det\Bigl(\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial u_{i}}\Bigl|\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial u_{j}}\Bigr.\Bigr)}=\sqrt{\det\Bigl(\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial v_{k}}\Bigl|\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial v_{l}}\Bigr.\Bigr)}\,\Bigl|\frac{dv}{du}\Bigr|,

whence

\int_{U}\sqrt{\det\Bigl(\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial u_{i}}\Bigl|\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial u_{j}}\Bigr.\Bigr)}\,du=\int_{V}\sqrt{\det\Bigl(\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial v_{k}}\Bigl|\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial v_{l}}\Bigr.\Bigr)}\,dv.

Definition 9.1
.

The last property of extent density allows us to define the
surface integral

of a function
f
on
M\subset\text{\ym R}^{d}
in a coordinate-free fashion by

\int_{M}f(x)\,|dx|_{M}=\int_{U}f(\phi(u))\sqrt{\det\Bigl(\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial u_{i}}\Bigl|\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial u_{j}}\Bigr.\Bigr)}\,du,

where the notation indicates that it is based on a measure
|\cdot|_{M}
in
M
.

Let
I_{\phi}
be a preintegral on
C_{c}(U)
defined by

I_{\phi}(g)=\int_{U}g(u)\sqrt{\det\Bigl(\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial u_{i}}\Bigl|\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial u_{j}}\Bigr.\Bigr)}\,du

with its Daniell extension denoted by
I_{\phi}^{1}:L^{1}(U,\phi)\to\text{\ym R}
.

Since parametrization-independence in the surface integral is based on the Jacobian formula,
the integrability of a function on
M
(
surface-integrability
) has a meaning and the set
L^{1}(M)
of surface-integrable functions turns out to be a linear lattice isomorphic to
L^{1}(U,\phi)
.

Example 9.2
.

(i)

For a smooth curve
C\subset\text{\ym R}^{d}
parametrized by
x=\phi(t)
(
a<t<b
) with
m=1
,

\int_{C}|dx|_{C}=\int_{a}^{b}\left|\frac{d\phi}{dt}\right|\,dt

is the length of
C
.

(ii)

For a smooth surface
M\subset\text{\ym R}^{d}
parametrized by
x=\phi(s,t)
with
(s,t)\in U\subset\text{\ym R}^{2}
,

\int_{M}|dx|_{M}=\int_{U}\sqrt{\Bigl(\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial s}\Bigl|\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial s}\Bigr.\Bigr)\Bigl(\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial t}\Bigl|\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial t}\Bigr.\Bigr)-\Bigl(\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial s}\Bigl|\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial t}\Bigr.\Bigr)^{2}}\,dsdt.

When
d=3
and
\phi
is denoted by
\phi(s,t)=(x(s,t),y(s,t),z(s,t))
, the extent density takes the form

\Bigl|\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial s}\times\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial t}\Bigr|=\\
\sqrt{\Bigl(\frac{\partial y}{\partial s}\frac{\partial z}{\partial t}-\frac{\partial z}{\partial s}\frac{\partial y}{\partial t}\Bigr)^{2}+\Bigl(\frac{\partial z}{\partial s}\frac{\partial x}{\partial t}-\frac{\partial x}{\partial s}\frac{\partial z}{\partial t}\Bigr)^{2}+\Bigl(\frac{\partial x}{\partial s}\frac{\partial y}{\partial t}-\frac{\partial y}{\partial s}\frac{\partial x}{\partial t}\Bigr)^{2}}.

(iii)

Let
\varphi
be a continuously differentiable function of
u\in U
with
U
an open subset of
\text{\ym R}^{d-1}

and consider a
(d-1)
-dimesional surface
M=\{(\varphi(u),u);u\in U\}
in
\text{\ym R}^{d}
with
\phi(u)=(\varphi(u),u)
. Then

\det(\partial_{j}\phi|\partial_{k}\phi)=\det(\phi^{\prime}){}^{t}(\phi^{\prime})=1+|\varphi^{\prime}|^{2}

by the Cauchy-Binet formula in Appendix
E

(or by a simple computation with rank-one operators) and the surface integral on
M
is described by

\int_{M}f(x)\,|dx|_{M}=\int_{U}f(\varphi(u),u))\sqrt{1+|\varphi^{\prime}(u)|^{2}}\,du.

Example 9.3
.

Consider a circle
(x-a)^{2}+z^{2}=b^{2}
(
0<b<a
) in the
xz
-plane and rotate it around the
z
-axis to get
a torus

(\sqrt{x^{2}+y^{2}}-a)^{2}+z^{2}=b^{2}
. To compute the surface area, we parametrize its upper half by

x=(a+b\sin\theta)\cos\varphi,\quad y=(a+b\sin\theta)\sin\varphi,\quad z=b\cos\theta

with

0\leq\varphi\leq 2\pi,\quad-\frac{\pi}{2}\leq\theta\leq\frac{\pi}{2}.

Then

\frac{\partial(x,y,z)}{\partial(\varphi,\theta)}=\begin{pmatrix}-(a+b\sin\theta)\sin\varphi&b\cos\theta\cos\varphi\\
(a+b\sin\theta)\cos\varphi&b\cos\theta\sin\varphi\\
0&-b\sin\theta\\
\end{pmatrix}

and

{\rule{0.0pt}{14.22636pt}}^{t}\left(\frac{\partial(x,y,z)}{\partial(\varphi,\theta)}\right)\left(\frac{\partial(x,y,z)}{\partial(\varphi,\theta)}\right)=\begin{pmatrix}(a+b\sin\theta)^{2}&0\\
0&b^{2}\end{pmatrix}

shows that the density is
b(a+b\sin\theta)
. Thus the toral surface area is

2b\int_{-\pi/2}^{\pi/2}(a+b\sin\theta)\,d\theta\int_{0}^{2\pi}d\varphi=2\pi a2\pi b.

.

C\subset\text{\ym R}^{3}
:
\phi(t)=(a\cos t,b\sin t,bt)
(

).

(d-1)

M=\{x\in\text{\ym R}^{d};x_{1}\geq 0,\dots,x_{d}\geq 0,x_{1}+\dots+x_{d}=1\}
in
\text{\ym R}^{d}

\sqrt{d}/(d-1)!
.

Exercise 44
.

Assume that
\phi:U\to M\subset\text{\ym R}^{d}
is a product of
an
m^{\prime}
-dimensional parametrization
\varphi:U^{\prime}\to M^{\prime}\subset\text{\ym R}^{d^{\prime}}
and
an
m^{\prime\prime}
-dimensional parametrization
\psi:U^{\prime\prime}\to M^{\prime\prime}\subset\text{\ym R}^{d^{\prime\prime}}
,
i.e.,
U=U^{\prime}\times U^{\prime\prime}
,
M=M^{\prime}\times M^{\prime\prime}
and
\phi(u)=(\varphi(u^{\prime}),\psi(u^{\prime\prime}))
for

u=(u^{\prime},u^{\prime\prime})\in\text{\ym R}^{m^{\prime}}\times\text{\ym R}^{m^{\prime\prime}}
.

Then

\int_{M}|dx|_{M}=\int_{M^{\prime}}|dx^{\prime}|_{M^{\prime}}\,\int_{M^{\prime\prime}}|dx^{\prime\prime}|_{M^{\prime\prime}}
with
(x^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime})\in\text{\ym R}^{d^{\prime}}\times\text{\ym R}^{d^{\prime\prime}}
.

Remark 13
.

Intuitively, a single coordinate parametrization is enough to almost cover
M
by removing lower dimensional negligible parts.

We shall now extend the construction so far for a single coordinate parametrization to the case of multiple parametrization.

Assume that
we are given a family of continuously differentiable one-to-one maps

\phi_{\alpha}:U_{\alpha}\to\text{\ym R}^{d}
(
U_{\alpha}
being an open subset of
\text{\ym R}^{m}
) so that
(i)
\text{rank}(\phi_{\alpha}^{\prime}(u):\text{\ym R}^{m}\to\text{\ym R}^{d})=m
for
u\in U_{\alpha}
,
(ii)
M=\bigcup_{\alpha}\phi_{\alpha}(U_{\alpha})
and (iii), if
\phi_{\alpha}(U_{\alpha})\cap\phi_{\beta}(U_{\beta})\not=\emptyset
,
the bijection
14
14

14

\phi_{\beta}^{-1}\phi_{\alpha}
is not a composite map but a single symbolic notation.

\phi_{\beta}^{-1}\phi_{\alpha}:\phi_{\alpha}^{-1}(\phi_{\beta}(U_{\beta}))\to\phi_{\beta}^{-1}(\phi_{\alpha}(U_{\alpha}))

defined by
\phi_{\alpha}(u)=\phi_{\beta}((\phi_{\beta}^{-1}\phi_{\alpha})(u))

(
u\in\phi_{\alpha}^{-1}(\phi_{\beta}(U_{\beta}))
) is continuously differentiable.
(The geomtric object
M\subset\text{\ym R}^{d}
is a so-called immersed submanifold.)

A one-to-one map
\varphi
of an open subset
U
of
\text{\ym R}^{m}
into
M
is then called
a
coordinate chart
of
M
if
both
\varphi^{-1}(\phi_{\alpha}(U_{\alpha}))=\{u\in U;\varphi(u)\in\phi_{\alpha}(U_{\alpha})\}
and

\phi_{\alpha}^{-1}(\varphi(U))=\{u\in U_{\alpha};\phi_{\alpha}(u)\in\varphi(U)\}
are open in
\text{\ym R}^{m}
with
the associated bijection
\phi_{\alpha}^{-1}\varphi:\varphi^{-1}(\phi_{\alpha}(U_{\alpha}))\to\phi_{\alpha}^{-1}(\varphi(U))

as well as its inverse map continuously differentiable for each
\phi_{\alpha}:U_{\alpha}\to\text{\ym R}^{d}
.
Here
\phi_{\alpha}^{-1}\varphi
is defined by
\phi_{\alpha}((\phi_{\alpha}^{-1}\varphi)(u))=\varphi(u)
(
u\in\varphi^{-1}(\phi_{\alpha}(U_{\alpha}))
).

Thus each map
U_{\alpha}\ni u\mapsto\phi_{\alpha}(u)\in M
is a coordinate chart and,
if
\psi:V\to M
is another coordinate chart,
the
coordinate transformation

\psi^{-1}\varphi:\varphi^{-1}(\psi(V))\ni u\mapsto v\in\psi^{-1}(\varphi(U))

defined by
\varphi(u)=\psi(v)
is a continuously differentiable bijection
from an open set
\varphi^{-1}(\psi(V))
in
\text{\ym R}^{m}
onto another open set
\psi^{-1}(\varphi(U))
in
\text{\ym R}^{m}
.

Figure 14.
Coordinate Transformation

Since open sets are Lebesgue measurable, so are their cuts and unions.
Moreover Lebesgue measurable sets are preserved under coordinate transformations (Proposition
8.6
),
which enables us to cut and union
L^{1}(U,\varphi)
for
various coordinate charts
\varphi:U\to M
to obtain a single space
L^{1}(M)
: The detailed construction is as follows.

Consider a function
f
on
M
which admits a finitely many coordinate charts
(\phi_{\alpha}:U_{\alpha}\to M\subset\text{\ym R}^{d})
satisfying

\phi_{\alpha}(U_{\alpha})f\in L^{1}(\phi_{\alpha}(U_{\alpha}))
for each
\alpha
and
(\bigcup_{\alpha}\phi_{\alpha}(U_{\alpha}))f=f
.

Lemma 9.4
.

(i)

We can find measurable sets
A_{\alpha}\subset U_{\alpha}
so that
\bigcup_{\alpha}\phi_{\alpha}(U_{\alpha})=\bigsqcup\phi_{\alpha}(A_{\alpha})

(a disjoint union).

(ii)

Let
\phi:U\to\bigcup_{\alpha}\phi_{\alpha}(U_{\alpha})\subset\text{\ym R}^{d}
be a coordinate chart of
M
.
Then
\phi(U)f\in L^{1}(\phi(U))
.

Proof.

(i) Write
\alpha=1,2,\dots,l
and let
A_{\alpha}
be defined by

\displaystyle A_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\phi_{\alpha}^{-1}\Bigl(\bigl(\phi_{1}(U_{1})\cup\dots\cup\phi_{\alpha}(U_{\alpha})\bigr)\setminus\bigl((\phi_{1}(U_{1})\cup\dots\cup\phi_{\alpha-1}(U_{\alpha-1})\bigr)\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\phi_{\alpha}^{-1}\Bigl(\phi_{\alpha}(U_{\alpha})\setminus\bigl((\phi_{1}(U_{1})\cup\dots\cup\phi_{\alpha-1}(U_{\alpha-1})\bigr)\Bigr)

\displaystyle=U_{\alpha}\setminus\phi_{\alpha}^{-1}\bigl((\phi_{1}(U_{1})\cup\dots\cup\phi_{\alpha-1}(U_{\alpha-1})\bigr),

which is Lebesgue measurable as a difference of open subsets.

(ii)
Since
U_{\alpha}\cap\phi_{\alpha}^{-1}\phi(U)
is Lebesgue measurable as an open subset, so is
A_{\alpha}\cap\phi_{\alpha}^{-1}\phi(U)
,
whence
A_{\alpha}\cap\phi_{\alpha}^{-1}\phi(U)(f\circ\phi_{\alpha})\sqrt{\det(\partial_{i}\phi_{\alpha}|\partial_{j}\phi)}
belongs to
L^{1}(U_{\alpha})

as a cut of
(f\circ\phi_{\alpha})\sqrt{\det(\partial_{i}\phi_{\alpha}|\partial_{j}\phi_{\alpha})}
by a Lebesgue measurable set and then,
by the Jacobian formula applied to

\phi^{-1}\phi_{\alpha}:U_{\alpha}\cap\phi_{\alpha}^{-1}\phi(U)\to U\cap\phi^{-1}\phi_{\alpha}(U_{\alpha})
,

(U\cap\phi^{-1}\phi_{\alpha}(A_{\alpha}))(f\circ\phi)
is Lebesgue integrable for each
\alpha
.
Consequently

U(f\circ\phi)\sqrt{\det(\partial_{i}\phi|\partial_{j}\phi)}=\sum_{\alpha}(U\cap\phi^{-1}\phi_{\alpha}(A_{\alpha}))(f\circ\phi)\sqrt{\det(\partial_{i}\phi|\partial_{j}\phi)}

belongs to
L^{1}(U)
, i.e.,
\phi(U)f\in L^{1}(\phi(U))
.
∎

Let
L(M)
be the totality of functions considered so far. Clearly
L(M)
is closed under lattice operations and in fact a linear lattice
in view of the above lemma.

Exercise 45
.

Show that
L(M)
is a linear space.

For
f\in L(M)
, choose
\varphi_{\alpha}:U_{\alpha}\to M
as before and measurable sets
A_{\alpha}\subset U_{\alpha}

so that
\bigcup_{\alpha}\varphi_{\alpha}(U_{\alpha})=\bigsqcup_{\alpha}\varphi_{\alpha}(A_{\alpha})
(Lemma
9.4
(i)).
A linear functional
I(f)
of
f\in L(M)
is then well-defined by

I(f)=\sum_{\alpha}I_{\varphi_{\alpha}}(A_{\alpha}(f\circ\varphi_{\alpha})).

In fact, for another choice
\psi_{\beta}:V_{\beta}\to M
with
B_{\beta}\subset V_{\beta}
covering
f
,

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha}I_{\varphi_{\alpha}}(A_{\alpha}(f\circ\varphi_{\alpha}))

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha,\beta}I_{\varphi_{\alpha}}((A_{\alpha}\cap\varphi_{\alpha}^{-1}(\psi_{\beta}(B_{\beta})))(f\circ\varphi_{\alpha}))

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha,\beta}I_{\psi_{\beta}}((\psi_{\beta}^{-1}(\varphi_{\alpha}(A_{\alpha}))\cap B_{\beta})(f\circ\psi_{\beta}))

\displaystyle=\sum_{\beta}I_{\psi_{\beta}}(B_{\beta}(f\circ\psi_{\beta})).

The linear functional
I(f)
is a preintegral because
f_{n}\downarrow 0
for
f_{n}\in L(M)
implies

\lim_{n\to\infty}I(f_{n})=\lim_{n\to\infty}\sum_{\alpha}I_{\varphi_{\alpha}}(A_{\alpha}(f_{n}\circ\varphi_{\alpha}))=\sum_{\alpha}\lim_{n\to\infty}I_{\varphi_{\alpha}}(A_{\alpha}(f_{n}\circ\varphi_{\alpha}))=0.

Let
I^{1}:L^{1}(M)\to\text{\ym R}
be the Daniell extension of
I
on
L(M)
, which contains
L^{1}(\varphi(U))
as a linear sublattice for
each coordinate chart
\varphi:U\to M
in such a way that

I^{1}(f)=I_{\varphi}^{1}(f\circ\varphi)
(
f\in L^{1}(\varphi(U))
) with
I^{1}(f)
reasonably denoted by

\int_{M}f(x)\,|dx|_{M}.

Exercise 46
.

Let
M\subset\text{\ym R}^{d}
be the product of
M^{\prime}\subset\text{\ym R}^{d^{\prime}}
and
M^{\prime\prime}\subset\text{\ym R}^{d^{\prime\prime}}
.
Then, for
f\in C_{c}(M)
, functions

x^{\prime}\mapsto\int_{M^{\prime\prime}}f(x^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime})\,|dx^{\prime\prime}|_{M^{\prime\prime}},\quad x^{\prime\prime}\mapsto\int_{M^{\prime}}f(x^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime})\,|dx^{\prime}|_{M^{\prime}}

belong to
C_{c}(M^{\prime})
and
C_{c}(M^{\prime\prime})
respectively for which the repeated integral formula holds:

\int_{M}f(x)\,|dx|_{M}=\int_{M^{\prime}}|dx^{\prime}|_{M^{\prime}}\int_{M^{\prime\prime}}f(x^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime})\,|dx^{\prime\prime}|_{M^{\prime\prime}}.

Density formula: The following is known as a smooth version of the
coarea formula
in geometric measure theory.

Let
\psi:D\ni x\mapsto v\in\text{\ym R}^{n}
(
D\subset\text{\ym R}^{d}
being an open set)
be a
submersion
15
15

15

Namely,
\psi
is continuously differentiable with
\text{rank}(\psi^{\prime}(x))=n
everywhere.

and
M
be a
level set

[\psi=v]
of
\psi
at
v\in\text{\ym R}^{n}
.
Let
f\in C_{c}(D)
be localized in a neighborhood of a point
a\in M\subset\text{\ym R}^{d}
.
Thanks to the inverse mapping theorem, after a suitable permutation of coordinates of
x
,
we may assume that
x\mapsto(u,v)
with
u=(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})
and

v=\psi(x)
is a local diffeomorphism in a neighborhood of
a
(
m+n=d
).
Here
diffeomorphism
is synonymous with smooth change-of-variables.

Then the inverse diffeomorphism is of the form
(u,v)\mapsto x=(u,\varphi(u,v))
and their differentials are given by

\frac{\partial x}{\partial(u,v)}=\begin{pmatrix}1_{m}&0\\
\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial u}&\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial v}\end{pmatrix},\quad\frac{\partial(u,v)}{\partial x}=\begin{pmatrix}1&&0&0&\dots&0\\
&\ddots&&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&&1&0&\dots&0\\
\frac{\partial v}{\partial x_{1}}&\dots&\frac{\partial v}{\partial x_{m}}&\frac{\partial v}{\partial x_{m+1}}&\dots&\frac{\partial v}{\partial x_{m+n}}\end{pmatrix}.

Since these are inverses of each other, we have

\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial v}\begin{pmatrix}\frac{\partial v}{\partial x_{m+1}}&\dots&\frac{\partial v}{\partial x_{m+n}}\end{pmatrix}=1_{n},\quad\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial u}+\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial v}\begin{pmatrix}\frac{\partial v}{\partial x_{1}}&\dots&\frac{\partial v}{\partial x_{m}}\end{pmatrix}=0.

Here
1_{m}
and
1_{n}
denote identity matrices of size
m
and
n
respectively.

As a local parametrization of level sets
[\psi=v]\subset\text{\ym R}^{d}
(
v
moving in a small open subset
V\subset\text{\ym R}^{n}
),
we can take one of the form
U\ni u\mapsto x=(u,\varphi(u,v))\in\text{\ym R}^{d}

(with
U
a neighborhood of
a\in\text{\ym R}^{m}
and
\varphi(u,v)
a continuously differentiable function of
(u,v)
)
so that the extent density is given by

\sqrt{\det\bigl(\delta_{i,j}+(\partial_{i}\varphi|\partial_{j}\varphi)\bigr)},\quad\partial_{i}\varphi=\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial u_{i}}(u,v)

and the surface integral of
f
on
[\psi=v]\subset D
by

\int_{[\psi=v]}f(x)\,|dx|_{[\psi=v]}=\int_{U}f(u,\varphi(u,v))\sqrt{\det\bigl(\delta_{i,j}+(\partial_{i}\varphi|\partial_{j}\varphi)\bigr)}\,du.

Lemma 9.5
.

Let
A
be an
m\times m
invertible matrix,
C
be an
n\times n
invertible matrix and
B
be an
n\times m
matrix.
We set
G=-C^{-1}BA^{-1}
so that

\begin{pmatrix}A&0\\
B&C\end{pmatrix}^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}A^{-1}&0\\
G&C^{-1}\end{pmatrix}.

Then

\det(A)^{-2}\det({}^{t}AA+{}^{t}BB)=\det(C)^{2}\det(G\,{}^{t}G+C^{-1\,}{}^{t}C^{-1}).

Proof.

Just compute as follows:

\displaystyle\det(A)^{-2}\det({}^{t}AA+{}^{t}BB)

\displaystyle=\det(1_{m}+{}^{t}(BA^{-1})(BA^{-1}))

\displaystyle=\det(1_{n}+(BA^{-1})\,{}^{t}(BA^{-1}))

\displaystyle=\det(1_{n}+(-CG)\,{}^{t}(-CG))

\displaystyle=\det(C)^{2}\det(G\,{}^{t}G+C^{-1\,}{}^{t}C^{-1}).

Here Sylvester’s formula (Appendix
E
) is used in the second line.
∎

We apply the above lemma for
A=1_{m}
,
B=\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial u}
and
C=\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial v}

with
(G\ C^{-1})=\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial x}
to get

\det\Bigl(\delta_{i,j}+\bigl(\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial u_{i}}|\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial u_{j}}\bigr)\Bigr)=\det\Bigl(\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial v}\Bigr)^{2}\det(\psi_{\imath}^{\prime}|\psi_{\jmath}^{\prime}),\quad(\psi_{\imath}^{\prime}|\psi_{\jmath}^{\prime})=\sum_{k=1}^{d}\frac{\partial\psi_{\imath}}{\partial x_{k}}\frac{\partial\psi_{\jmath}}{\partial x_{k}},

which is used to see

\displaystyle\int_{\psi^{-1}(V)}f(x)

\displaystyle\sqrt{\det(\psi_{\imath}^{\prime}|\psi_{\jmath}^{\prime})}\,dx

\displaystyle=\int_{U\times V}f(u,\varphi(u,v))\sqrt{\det(\psi_{\imath}^{\prime}|\psi_{\jmath}^{\prime})}\Bigl|\det\Bigl(\frac{\partial x}{\partial(u,v)}\Bigr)\Bigr|\,dudv

\displaystyle=\int_{U\times V}f(u,\varphi(u,v))\sqrt{\det(\psi_{\imath}^{\prime}|\psi_{\jmath}^{\prime})}\Bigl|\det\Bigl(\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial v}\Bigr)\Bigr|\,dudv

\displaystyle=\int_{V}dv\,\int_{U}f(u,\varphi(u,v))\sqrt{\det\Bigl(\delta_{i,j}+\bigl(\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial u_{i}}|\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial u_{j}}\bigr)\Bigr)}\,du

\displaystyle=\int_{V}dv\,\int_{[\psi=v]}f(x)\,|dx|_{[\psi=v]}.

Finally this localized identity is patched up globally,
this time by a partition of unity
16
16

16

A geometric form of Fubini theorem can be also used.
(Proposition
5.14
), to have the following.

Theorem 9.6
.

Given a submersion
\psi:\text{\ym R}^{d}\supset D\ni x\mapsto\psi(x)\in\text{\ym R}^{n}
and a function
f\in C_{c}(D)
, we have

\int_{D}f(x)\sqrt{\det(\psi_{\imath}^{\prime}|\psi_{\jmath}^{\prime})}\,dx=\int_{\psi(D)}dv\,\int_{[\psi=v]}f(x)\,|dx|_{[\psi=v]}.

Proof.

By the local formula, each point
a\in[f]
has an open neighborhood
W
such that the global forumla holds
if
f
belongs to
C_{c}(W)\subset C_{c}(D)
. From the finite covering property, we can find a finitely many such open sets
W_{\alpha}

so that
[f]\subset\bigcup W_{\alpha}
. We apply the partition of unity to this covering to get
h_{\alpha}\in C_{c}(W_{\alpha})
satisfying

\sum_{\alpha}h_{\alpha}=1
on
[f]
.

Then
f_{\alpha}=h_{\alpha}f\in C_{c}(W_{\alpha})
is summed to be
f
and we have

\displaystyle\int_{D}f(x)\sqrt{\det(\psi_{\imath}^{\prime}|\psi_{\jmath}^{\prime})}\,dx

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha}\int_{D}f_{\alpha}(x)\sqrt{\det(\psi_{\imath}^{\prime}|\psi_{\jmath}^{\prime})}\,dx

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha}\int_{\psi(D)}dv\,\int_{[\psi=v]}f_{\alpha}(x)\,|dx|_{[\psi=v]}

\displaystyle=\int_{\psi(D)}dv\,\int_{[\psi=v]}f(x)\,|dx|_{[\psi=v]}.

∎

Corollary 9.7
.

Let
M
be a level set
[\psi=v]
of
\psi
. Then

\lim_{V\to v}\frac{1}{|V|}\int_{\psi^{-1}(V)}f(x)\sqrt{\det(\psi_{\imath}^{\prime}|\psi_{\jmath}^{\prime})}\,dx=\int_{M}f(x)\,|dx|_{M}.

Remark 14
.

By rewriting surface integrals in terms of Hausdorff measure,
a further generalization is known as the coarea formula

in geometric measure theory (see
[
1
]
).

Example 9.8
.

Let
\psi:D=\text{\ym R}^{d}\to\text{\ym R}^{n}
be a linear map,
which is identified with an
n\times d
matrix, and assume that the cut of
\psi
by the last
n
columns
is invertible as an
n\times n
matrix.
Then local coordinates
(u,v)\in\text{\ym R}^{m+n}
satisfying

\begin{pmatrix}u\\
v\end{pmatrix}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c c}I_{m}&0\\
\lx@intercol\hfil\psi\hfil\lx@intercol\end{array}\right)x\iff x=\begin{pmatrix}I_{m}&0\\
B&C\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}u\\
v\end{pmatrix}\ \text{with}\ \varphi(u,v)=Bu+Cv

provides global one and the equality of

\int_{D}f(x)\sqrt{\det((\psi_{i}^{\prime}|\psi_{j}^{\prime}))}\,dx=\int_{\text{\ym R}^{d}}f(x)\sqrt{\det(\psi{}^{t}\psi)}\,dx

and

\int_{\psi(D)}dv\int_{[\psi=v]}F(x)\,|dx|_{[\psi=v]}=\int_{\text{\ym R}^{n}}dv\int_{\text{\ym R}^{m}}f(u,\varphi(u,v))|\det(C)|\sqrt{\det(\psi{}^{t}\psi)}\,du

is reduced to the identity

\int_{\text{\ym R}^{d}}f(x)\,dx=|\det(C)|\int_{\text{\ym R}^{n}}dv\int_{\text{\ym R}^{m}}f(u,\varphi(u,v))\,du,

which is nothing but a combination of the (linear) Jacobian formula and repeated integrals.

Example 9.9
.

Let
\psi(x)=|x|
for
0\not=x\in\text{\ym R}^{d}
(
D=\text{\ym R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
,
n=1
). Then
\psi^{\prime}(x)=\frac{x}{|x|}
and

\displaystyle\int_{D}f(x)\,dx

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}dr\int_{rS^{d-1}}f(x)\,|dx|_{rS^{d-1}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}dr\,r^{d-1}\int_{S^{d-1}}f(r\omega)\,|d\omega|_{S^{d-1}}.

Now, for the choice
f(x)=e^{-|x|^{2}}
,

\int_{\text{\ym R}^{d}}e^{-|x|^{2}}\,dx=\int_{D}e^{-|x|^{2}}\,dx=|S^{d-1}|\int_{0}^{\infty}r^{d-1}e^{-r^{2}}\,dr.

The left hand side is equal to
\pi^{d/2}
as a multiple Gaussian integral and the integral in the right hand side is
expressed in terms of the gamma function by
\Gamma(d/2)/2
, resulting in the
spherical integral

|S^{d-1}|=\frac{2\pi^{d/2}}{\Gamma(d/2)}.

With this formula in hand,
f(x)=e^{-|x|^{\beta}}/|x|^{\alpha}
for
\alpha\in\text{\ym R}
and
\beta>0
is then calculated as follows:

\displaystyle\int_{\text{\ym R}^{d}}\frac{e^{-|x|^{\beta}}}{|x|^{\alpha}}\,dx=|S^{d-1}|\int_{0}^{\infty}r^{d-1}\frac{e^{-r^{\beta}}}{r^{\alpha}}\,dr

\displaystyle=|S^{d-1}|\frac{1}{\beta}\int_{0}^{\infty}s^{-1+(d-\alpha)/\beta}e^{-s}\,dt

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\frac{2\pi^{d/2}}{\beta\Gamma(d/2)}\Gamma(\frac{d-\alpha}{\beta})&(\alpha<d),\\
\infty&(\alpha\geq d).\end{cases}

Check that
|S^{0}|=2
,
|S^{1}|=2\pi
and
|S^{2}|=4\pi
.

V_{d}

Remark 15
.

Square-rooted determinant densities are closely related to the Jacobian.

To see this, consider a smooth map
\varphi:U\to\text{\ym R}^{n}
defined on an open set
U\subset\text{\ym R}^{m}

with
\varphi^{\prime}(x)
a matrix-valued function of size
n\times m
.
Then
\det(\partial_{i}\varphi|\partial_{j}\phi)=\det({}^{t}\varphi^{\prime}(x)\varphi^{\prime}(x))
and

\det(\varphi_{i}^{\prime}(x)|\varphi_{j}^{\prime}(x))=\det(\varphi^{\prime}(x)\,{}^{t}\varphi^{\prime}(x))
,
whence these coinside by Sylvester’s formula (Appendix
E
).

When
m=n
, they are reduced to
\det(\varphi^{\prime}(x))^{2}
.
In accordance with this fact, their square roots are also called the Jacobian of
\varphi
.

We here restrict ourselves to the case
n=1
;

\psi
is a scalar function and the level set
[\psi=v]
is a hypersurface
in
\text{\ym R}^{d}
.
In the local coordinate expression

\int_{[\psi=v]}f(x)\,|dx|_{\psi=v}=\int_{U}f(x)\left|\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial v}\right||\psi^{\prime}(x)|\,du\quad(x=(u,\varphi(u,v))),

notice that the density function is equal to the norm of a vector

(-\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial u},1)=\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial v}\psi^{\prime}=\frac{1}{\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial x_{d}}}\psi^{\prime},

which is normal to the hypersurface
[\psi=v]
at the point
x=(u,\varphi(u,v))\in[\psi=v]
.
With the aid of the normal unit vector

\bm{n}(x)=\frac{1}{|\psi^{\prime}(x)|}\psi^{\prime}(x)

pointing to the direction of increasing
\psi
, we introduce
a vector-valued measure (so-called
surface element
)
by

dx_{[\psi=v]}=\bm{n}(x)|dx|_{[\psi=v]}

and, for a continuous vector field
F(x)\in\text{\ym R}^{d}
(
x\in D
) of compact support,
define the
surface integral
17
17

17

Also called the
flux
of a vector field
F

through the hypersurface
[\psi=v]
.
of
F
on the hypersurface
[\psi=v]
by

\int_{[\psi=v]}F(x)\cdot dx_{[\psi=v]}=\int_{[\psi=v]}F(x)\cdot\bm{n}(x)\,|dx|_{[\psi=v]}.

In local coordinates,

\bm{n}(x)=\frac{\epsilon}{\sqrt{1+|\partial\varphi/\partial u|^{2}}}\Bigl(-\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial u},1\Bigr)\quad\text{with}\quad\epsilon=\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial v}/\left|\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial v}\right|\in\{\pm 1\}

and

\int_{[\psi=v]}F(x)\cdot dx_{[\psi=v]}=\int_{U}F(x)\cdot\psi^{\prime}(x)\left|\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial v}\right|\,du\quad(x=(u,\varphi(u,v))).

When
F(x)=f(x)\bm{n}(x)
, this is reduced to the surface integral of the scalar function
f
.

Figure 15.
Flux

Now assume that
F
is continuously differentiable on
D
.
If the compact support of
F
is contained in an open set
W\subset\text{\ym R}^{d}
for which
W\simeq U\times V
by a local coordinate description
of
\psi
with
U\subset\text{\ym R}^{d-1}
an open rectangle and
V\subset\text{\ym R}
an open interval, we claim

\frac{d}{dv}\int_{[\psi=v]}F(x)\cdot dx_{[\psi=v]}=\int_{[\psi=v]}\frac{f(x)}{|\psi^{\prime}(x)|}\,|dx|_{[\psi=v]}.

Here
f
is the
divergence
of
F
:

f(x)=\text{div}F=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\frac{\partial F_{i}}{\partial x_{i}}.

In fact, in local coordinates, the formula takes the form

\frac{d}{dv}\int_{U}\epsilon F\cdot(-\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial u},1)\,du=\int_{U}\epsilon f(x)\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial v}\,du\quad(x=(u,\varphi(u,v))).

From the chain rule relation

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial v}\left(F\cdot(-\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial u},1)\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial}{\partial v}\left(-\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}F_{i}\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial u_{i}}+F_{d}\right)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}\frac{\partial F_{i}}{\partial x_{d}}\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial v}\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial u_{i}}-\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}F_{i}\frac{\partial^{2}\varphi}{\partial v\partial u_{i}}+\frac{\partial F_{d}}{\partial x_{d}}\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial v}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}\left(\frac{\partial F_{i}}{\partial x_{d}}\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial v}\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial u_{i}}+F_{i}\frac{\partial^{2}\varphi}{\partial v\partial u_{i}}+\frac{\partial F_{i}}{\partial x_{i}}\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial v}\right)+f\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial v}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}\frac{\partial}{\partial u_{i}}(F_{i}\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial v})+f\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial v},

the difference of the local formula therefore amounts to

\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}\int_{U}\frac{\partial}{\partial u_{i}}(F_{i}\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial v})\,du,

which vanishes by repeated integral expressions in view of the fact that

F_{i}\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial v}
vanishes at the boundary of
U
:

\displaystyle\int_{U}\frac{\partial}{\partial u_{i}}(F_{i}\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial v})\,du

\displaystyle=\int_{U_{i}}(du)_{i}\int_{a_{i}}^{b_{i}}\frac{\partial}{\partial u_{i}}(F_{i}\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial v})\,du_{i}

\displaystyle=\int_{U_{i}}\left(\left.F_{i}\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial v}\right|_{u_{i}=b_{i}}-\left.F_{i}\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial v}\right|_{u_{i}=a_{i}}\right)\,(du)_{i}=0.

Here
U=(a_{1},b_{1})\times\dots\times(a_{d-1},b_{d-1})
,

U_{i}=(a_{1},b_{1})\times\dots\times(a_{i-1},b_{i-1})\times(a_{i+1},b_{i+1})\times\dots\times(a_{d-1},b_{d-1})

and
(du)_{i}=du_{1}\cdots du_{i-1}du_{i+1}\cdots du_{d-1}
.

The local formula is now glued together by a partition of unity (cf. the proof of Theorem
9.6
) to obtain the global formula.

Theorem 9.10
.

Let
\psi:D\to\text{\ym R}
be a submersion and

F\in C_{c}^{1}(D,\text{\ym R}^{d})
be a continuously differentiable vector field of compact support on
D
.
Then

\frac{d}{dv}\int_{[\psi=v]}F(x)\cdot dx_{[\psi=v]}=\int_{[\psi=v]}\frac{\text{div}\,F(x)}{|\psi^{\prime}(x)|}\,|dx|_{[\psi=v]}

for
v\in\psi(D)
.

Combined with the coarea formula (Theorem
9.6
), we have the following.

Corollary 9.11

(Divergence Theorem
18
18

18

A flow version of divergence theorem can be found in
[
4
,
8
]
.
)
.

Let
[a,b]\subset\psi(D)
. Then

\int_{[a\leq\psi\leq b]}\text{div}\,F(x)\,dx=\int_{[\psi=b]}F(x)\cdot dx_{[\psi=b]}-\int_{[\psi=a]}F(x)\cdot dx_{[\psi=a]}.

Figure 16.
Flux Flow

As a limit case, consider the situation where
\psi(D)=(0,c)
and
[\psi=v]
approaches to a closed set

[\psi=0]
as
v\to+0
in such a way that
[\psi=0]\subset\partial D
,
[0\leq\psi<b]=[\psi=0]\sqcup[\psi<b]
is an open set of
\text{\ym R}^{d}
for any
0<b<c
and

\lim_{v\to+0}\int_{[\psi=v]}f(x)\,|dx|_{[\psi=v]}=0\quad(f\in C_{c}^{+}([\psi\geq 0])).

The above flow version
is then filled with the inner boundary
[\psi=0]
to get the boundary version

\int_{[0\leq\psi<b]}\text{div}\,F(x)\,dx=\int_{[\psi=b]}F(x)\cdot dx_{\partial\widetilde{D}}\quad(F\in C_{c}^{1}([\psi\geq 0],\text{\ym R}^{d})).

Example 9.13

(Sphere)
.

Let
\psi(x)=|x|
and

\bm{n}(x)=x/|x|
(
x\in D

and, for
